I switched from the finance industry to the energy industry via the carbon markets back in 2005 . <p> When the Kyoto Protocol was ratified in 2005 , a few other colleagues and I genuinely thought that carbon market mechanism would be going to be a very interesting tool to use as a means to emissions reductions . Which is what it was intended to be back then . Therefore I started a business that was trying to capture pollution and turn it into income streams , taking advantage of the United Nations framework at the time , which was called the Clean Development Mechanism , which still exists to this day . <p> At the time , the carbon industry was supposed to be around until 2020 when industry and governments were supposed to move from carbon certificates to actual emissions reductions -- that was the intent . <p> The next step was to ask:if you are going to start a business that is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ stream , where is that pollution ? Most of that pollution was coming from , in volume terms , China , India , Southeast Asia , and the United States . And so we focused on methane and set up businesses that were capturing methane gas from oil and gas and coal mines principally , effectively then piping them Into containers to generate electricity from them . That 's how I started in the industry . <p> But that particular carbon market , the Clean Development Mechanism , collapsed in 2013 a few years after the global financial crisis of 2008 . We therefore pivoted from carbon to renewable energy because the portfolio had renewable energy assets within it . <p> That 's how the past 20 years developed . Today , it 's really just renewable energy . And it 's been just renewable energy for over 10 years now , because , on the one hand , the carbon market collapsed , but on the other hand , I think the innocence associated with the early days of the carbon markets has been replaced with an increased realisation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ really -- that actual emission reductions simply were not happening , and that the carbon markets were being used as an excuse to either greenwash or avoid the topic altogether . <h> What are some of the challenges in scaling the business ? For example , a country like Indonesia has got many structural hurdles to get around . National utility PLN limits the renewables that can be added to the grid . <p> That 's a big question . First of all , there are four Asias -- China , India , North Asia ex-China , and Southeast Asia -- and each is quite distinct in terms of how you might approach the market . I 've started businesses in all of them . <p> I 'm not sure there is an issue , per se , scaling in any of these markets . The business , whether it 's carbon or renewable energy , is fundamentally a very local business . As long as you have set up a local operation , the operation can scale . Every market has its own particularities . I 'm not sure @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is any easier than doing business in any of the Asian markets that I have experience in . <p> But you have to localise . It 's really about people . If you 've got the talent that you need in these countries , there is so much that you can do because the space is not that occupied . <h> Southeast Asia is the only region here coal is growing in its energy mix . Where do you see the key opportunities for renewable energy growth in Southeast Asia ? <p> Let 's take a step back . Today , we are in a world that is significantly different from the world as it was 10 years ago . The reason we are in a world that is so fundamentally different is because of solar power . Now , we finally figured out how to harness the sun cost-effectively and how to scale solar power . What you therefore see around you right now is those 18 terawatts of power on their way to becoming 40 , 60 , and maybe 100 TW of power . There 's a massive scale up of the energy at our disposal . <p> This has never happened before in the history of humanity . We 've always lived energy constrained since the dawn of time . The nearest earthquake zone to the area is in Sumatra , around 400km from Singapore @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with the Academy of Sciences Malaysia ( ASM ) told CNA that the location of the epicentre of the two earthquakes on Sunday reinforces the point that the fault is " indeed active " . <p> " The alignment of the incident is also consistent with regional fault patterns ( roughly northwest -- southeast ) , suggesting the possible reactivation of an old fault , " said the earthquake engineering expert . <p> He added that records of earthquakes due to major faults in the peninsula indicate that tremors with magnitudes of up to 5 have occurred periodically , and even magnitude six in rare cases . <p> " Considering the Mersing Fault ( Zone ) , which could be at least 20km long , if it ruptures in full , a magnitude of up to magnitude 6.5 could occur . @@128614092 <p> IN A world worried about looming food shortages triggered by the climate crisis , the collapse in rice prices -- now approaching their lowest in 18 years -- is evidence that interventions by governments and modern agricultural methods may save the day . The key is productivity : more food from fewer farmers . <p> When we think about technological advances , what comes to mind are the Internet , smartphones and now the arrival of artificial intelligence . But farming has enjoyed a dramatic and often overlooked productivity revolution : Over the last century , crop yields have exploded . <p> Rice is a great example . In 1975 , farmers around the world harvested an average of 2.4 tonnes per hectare ; the yield improved to 3.8 tonnes by 2000 , and today it 's almost doubled to 4.7 tonnes . <p> Other crops , from corn to soybean to wheat , have also experienced massive gains , allowing larger crops even in more difficult climate conditions . And those gains can be sustained . <p> The corn market is also paradigmatic of our newfound ability to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , yields flatlined at about 20 to 30 bushels per acre . From 1945 onward , they 've steadily improved , first at a rate of about one bushel per acre every year , and more recently at a rate of about two per year . <p> This year , the US Department of Agriculture expects farmers to reap nearly 189 bushels per acre -- a record , and double what was possible 50 years ago . <h> BT in your inbox <p> Start and end each day with the latest news stories and analyses delivered straight to your inbox . <p> We 're all benefiting from that little-known productivity boom : Without it , food prices would be significantly higher , and larger swathes of the world would regularly go hungry . <p> Still , opposition to modern farming methods keeps increasing , often accompanied by calls for a return to yesteryear 's ways : A world with little mechanised farming , without fertilisers , pesticides , genetically modified seeds or irrigation . <p> The benefits of government investments in modern farming are most evident in rice , the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ if largely ignored by investors and Wall Street , rice is a staple for half the world 's population . <p> Over the last four years , Asian and African nations had been on edge : A dangerous mix of bad weather , protectionism and panic buying fuelled an inflationary spike in prices . <p> In Asia , the grain has the potential to topple governments ; national leaders watched with apprehension as the regional benchmark , Thai 5 per cent white rice , surged to a 10-year high of US$650 a tonne by early 2024 , up more than 60 per cent from US$400 a tonne in mid 2021 . <p> The concern was that prices could revert to the record high of more than US$1,000 a tonne seen in 2007 to 2008 , when food riots spread from Bangladesh to Senegal to Haiti . <p> Some feared that this was the new normal , due to the impact of climate change on crops . Certainly , the weather was at play ; but rather than the climate crisis , the main culprit was the on-and-off El Nino weather @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that carbon emissions would make rice perennially more expensive proved overblown . <p> Now that rain has returned to most of Asia , modern farming is helping . The world will reap a record rice harvest of about 541 million tonnes in 2025 to 2026 . For perspective , that would be double the crop of 1980 to 1981 , while the amount of land in cultivation has changed little . <h> Credit access and public investment <p> No wonder prices are down . The world -- and Asia in particular -- can do more to extend the productivity boom . The key is ensuring that farmers have plentiful access to credit , so they can invest in modern machinery , fertilisers and pesticides . <p> Irrigation is also essential , and that demands public investment , which should also be channelled into research to improve seeds . Advances in agricultural genetics , which can create plants that tolerate both less rainfall and flooding , should be encouraged , not banned . <p> Chinese scientists have completed trials of new genetically modified rice varieties that offer much hope ; others @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ technological advance , there are risks . Over-fertilisation is one , but that can be tackled by educating farmers to adopt the best techniques . <p> In the meantime , genetically modified organism ( GMO ) seeds pose risks to neither health nor the environment , in my opinion ; they 've been banned in much of the world for no good reason . <p> In a 2010 report , the European Commission reviewed more than 25 years of scientific research on GMO , concluding that " biotechnology , and in particular GMOs , are not per se more risky than conventional plant breeding technologies " . <p> The United Nations World Health Organization has said that after several decades of GMO cultivation in several countries , " the consumption of GM foods has not caused any known negative health effects " . <p> There 's a final challenge : More productive farming ultimately means fewer farmers . And that 's a good thing -- Asia and Africa need more food , not more people working the land . <p> Governments need to manage migration from rural areas to cities @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ or the service economy -- the path to riches in America and Europe over the last 100 years . <p> As the price of rice shows , science can help the world to cope with the changing climate without going hungry . @@128614337 <p> In May , a woman in Wuhan knocked CNY 90 ( US$12.53 ) off her monthly mortgage repayment using a personal carbon-accounting app , Xinhua explained . <p> Wei Ying had accumulated the credits on an account run by the municipal government . Wuhan residents can gain points by using low-carbon modes of transport , with a bus trip garnering 212.5 grams of emissions reductions . Every kilometre on a subway gets 78.4 grams , and on a bike 93.3 grams . These credits can then be exchanged for coupons on taxis , groceries , shopping and , until the end of 2025 , home mortgages . <p> Wei Ying needed 45,000 grams of credits to get that CNY 90 off her mortgage . With another 20,000 grams of credits , she got a discount on wine . <p> Such platforms are part of China 's " carbon inclusivity " sector , which incentivises individuals to tally low-carbon behaviours and convert them into a form of carbon credits . PHOTO : AFP <p> August 29 , 2025 <p> DHAKA -- Air pollution is cutting Bangladeshis ' average life expectancy by 5.5 years , making it the country 's deadliest external health risk , according to a report published yesterday . <p> The global annual report of the Air Quality Life Index ( AQLI ) , published by the University of Chicago 's Energy Policy Institute ( EPIC ) , warns that health toll caused by particulate matter ( PM ) pollution far exceeds that of tobacco use , malnutrition , or unsafe water . Smoking reduces life by about two years , malnutrition by 1.4 . <p> " Bangladeshis are losing more years of life to dirty air than to smoking or malnutrition , " said Michael Greenstone , director of EPIC and creator of the AQLI . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ residents are exposed to fine particulate pollution above both the World Health Organization ( WHO ) guideline of five micrograms per cubic meter and the national standard of 35 . <p> Between 1998 and 2023 , the country 's PM2.5 pollution -- particles smaller than 2.5 micrometres -- surged by 66 percent , eroding 2.4 years of life expectancy . In 2023 , annual average PM2.5 rose to 60.8 micrograms , 1.7 times the national standard and more than 12 times the WHO guideline . <p> " If PM2.5 concentrations in Bangladesh were reduced to meet WHO limits , an average resident could live 5.5 years longer , " the report states . <p> Dhaka faces one of the gravest impacts , with residents potentially living nearly seven years longer under WHO standards . Meeting the weaker national standard would still add over four years . <p> In Chattogram , residents could gain 6.2 years under WHO limits and 3.3 years under the national threshold . <p> Together , Dhaka and Chattogram -- home to nearly half the country 's population -- would see average life expectancy rise by over @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> In Gazipur , residents could gain 7.1 years with cleaner air , while Narayanganj residents could add 6.6 years . In Comilla , Tangail , Khulna , and Mymensingh , the gains would range between five and six years , and in Sylhet 3.5 years . <p> The AQLI 's 2023 data shows global PM2.5 concentrations were 1.5 percent higher than that of 2022 . Its toll on life expectancy is four times that of alcohol use , and five times that of transport injuries or unsafe water and sanitation . <p> In 2022 , Bangladesh 's Ministry of Environment , Forest and Climate Change formulated the Air Pollution Control Rules . These require a National Air Quality Control Plan , nationwide monitoring , identification of pollution-causing activities , and emission standards for industry , automobiles , and major projects . <p> In Dhaka , the 2019 brick kiln law bars kilns near residential , commercial , agricultural , and sensitive areas . <p> As a punitive step , the government last week declared Savar a " degraded airshed " and said operations of all brick kilns , except @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from next month . <p> Sources of PM2.5 include transboundary pollution , brick kilns , smoke from low-grade fuels , solid waste burning , and dust from construction . Brick kilns around Dhaka alone cause 58 percent of the city 's air pollution . On February 24 , the High Court ordered the removal of all illegal brick kilns nationwide . <p> Speaking to The Daily Star , Environment Adviser Syeda Rizwana Hasan said 3,491 kilns without clearance would be shut down , and illegal hill kilns relocated . " We have already instructed closure of illegal kilns and are conducting drives at construction sites . @@128703555 <p> WASHINGTON DC : Ten years on from the landmark Paris Agreement on climate change , 2025 could have been a fitting anniversary -- with countries updating their Nationally Determined Contributions ( NDCs ) and setting out 2035 emission targets . <p> Instead , 2025 has proven a difficult year for climate ambition . Trade disputes , energy security concerns and the resurgence of nationalist politics have distracted major economies from climate commitments . United States President Donald Trump has not just actively rolled back climate policies but has now threatened tariffs against countries that support an international agreement for a carbon tax on shipping emissions . <p> At an upcoming United Nations climate summit on Sep 24 , key countries are expected to outline their 2035 emissions targets that will help set the floor for international ambition over the next decade . However , it will also spotlight a sobering reality : The scale of effort required to keep global warming in check remains immense , while political willingness to meet that challenge appears unprecedentedly strained . <p> Against this backdrop , the upcoming United Nations General Assembly ( @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reckoning . <p> Developments in the world 's three most consequential economies -- the United States , European Union and China -- offer a window into shared political challenges and diverging capacities to sustain long-term progress , depending on their ability to align climate goals with economic strategy in this new phase of global climate action . <h> AMERICAN U-TURN AND EU STRAIN <p> In the US , Mr Trump has overseen a dramatic U-turn of predecessor Joe Biden 's climate policy at home and severely eroded American credibility in international climate negotiations . <p> His administration has dismantled clean energy incentives , injected severe uncertainties to the clean energy market and rolled back regulations -- from challenging the fundamental legal basis of regulating carbon emissions to de-funding federal agencies critical to addressing the climate challenge . <p> Internationally , the US has also withdrawn from climate diplomacy forums and will slash funding for multilateral climate efforts . <p> Across the Atlantic , climate policy in the European Union appears far more stable . Yet , under the strain of security challenges and a persistent rightward political shift , even @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the overarching framework of the European Green Deal -- the bloc 's flagship climate initiative -- remains , several of its core elements have been softened . <h> Related : <p> These include the weakening of corporate sustainability obligations , delays to methane control measures and the loosening of vehicle emissions standards . Under the banner of " bureaucratic simplification " , further retreats are unfolding in areas such as deforestation , biodiversity protection and sustainable finance . <h> MODEST POLITICAL WILL BUT RISING COMMERCIAL POWER IN CHINA <p> For some time , there was hope that China would emerge as a proactive international climate voice , even if it would not or could not fill the space vacated by the US . Clean tech firms are rapidly expanding overseas too , gaining momentum in Global South markets . <h> Related : <p> Yet , despite this economic dynamism , Beijing 's political will on climate remains modest . <p> In response to an economic slowdown and worsening geopolitical environment , top leaders have dialled back high-profile climate rhetoric and sidelined major emissions targets . The government work report released in March during China 's annual Two Sessions political meeting carried no mention of targets to reduce carbon intensity -- downplaying one of its headline commitments under the Paris Agreement . <p> These national dynamics will inevitably shape the tone and outcomes of climate deliberations at the UNGA . <p> The US , on its way out of the Paris Agreement , is retreating . Europe remains internally fragmented and uncertain in presenting a unified front in September . Only 15 of the 195 Paris Agreement signatories did so by the initial February deadline . A clear gap will remain between what countries are willing to commit to and what climate science demands . <p> Still , there is some cause for cautious hope . There is growing recognition that a nation 's climate ambition can no longer be assessed solely by the strength of its political declarations and this should help guide assessment of the UNGA . <p> The credibility and impact of climate policy will hinge on how deeply it is embedded in the real economy : through investment flows , industrial strategies , supply chain alignment and trade policies . <p> Seen through this lens , Beijing seems better-positioned to win the long game . <p> The 2025 UNGA will not deliver all the answers , but it will highlight the gap between rhetoric and reality . ( Photo : CNA/Ooi Boon Keong ) <p> While it might be easy enough to dash into an air-conditioned space to cool down or switch on an extra fan when one is feeling the heat , such quick fixes drive up energy consumption and greenhouse gas emissions , ultimately worsening the very problem people are trying to escape from . <p> The challenge , experts said , is not to avoid the heat but to adapt to it . This requires changes both in infrastructure and in the choices individuals make every day . <p> In Singapore , scientists , urban planners and ordinary residents are exploring new ways to cope , which include rethinking how buildings are designed and experimenting with technologies that can ease the strain of heat stress on our bodies . <p> From a tile made out of fungi and bamboo shavings to a cooling vest designed for athletes and outdoor workers exposed to high @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the innovations -- and the obstacles in the way of their implementation -- as Singapore gears up for life in a hotter world . <h> Related : <h> DESIGNING WITH NATURE <p> As heat hacks such as turning up the air-conditioning are not sustainable , the real long-term solution lies in how we design and build our cities , experts say . <p> Some of the most promising solutions are being tested in laboratories today , and reflected in how developers shape malls , offices and public spaces across Singapore . <p> For example , a team of scientists from Nanyang Technological University ( NTU ) and local ecology and biomimicry design firm bioSEA are working on an idea for cooling buildings : " fungi tiles " that have the texture of elephant skin . <p> The tiles , designed by NTU Associate Professor Hortense Le Ferrand , bioSEA 's Dr Anuj Jain , and NTU researchers Dr Teoh Jia Heng and Eugene Soh , are made from mycelium -- the root network of fungi . Using strains of mycelium from oyster and Lingzhi mushrooms , the mycelium is grown @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ furniture shop , oats and water , then packed into a hexagonal mold textured to resemble elephant skin . <p> " Fungi tiles " that have the texture of elephant skin can potentially be a sustainable alternative to thermal insulation made from polystyrene that are commonly used to cool buildings today . ( Photo : CNA/Ooi Boon Keong ) <p> " How elephants cool themselves is that they have a lot of grooves and wrinkles in their skin , which creates these air channels that help to increase surface area , and that helps them to release all the heat that is trapped , " said Mr Soh . <p> " You also see elephants spraying themselves with water , so they are also trapping these water droplets within these grooves and wrinkles , which also helps with evaporative cooling . " <p> Dr Teoh the researcher said the " fungi tiles " have a circular life cycle as they can biodegrade or be reused to grow new composites . This makes the tiles a potential sustainable alternative to thermal insulation made from polystyrene , a material commonly used in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in landfills once discarded . <p> Others are already deploying cutting-edge technology to make Singapore 's built environment greener . <p> Industrial master planner and developer JTC said its estates ' structures are planned to be naturally cooler , lowering reliance on air-conditioning , cutting energy costs and reducing the estate 's overall carbon footprint . <p> In the layout for the Punggol Digital District , for example , JTC used advanced computational simulations to model wind flow and solar radiation . It then oriented buildings to maximise natural ventilation and minimise solar heat gain . <p> Buildings there are predominantly oriented on a north-south axis , and many are elevated at ground level to create naturally ventilated , shaded public spaces , said a spokesperson . <p> The Punggol Digital District , slated to be fully completed by 2026 , also includes sensor-driven ventilation , using high-volume , low-speed fans in public areas that are automatically activated by weather sensors , thus operating only when needed . <p> The Punggol Digital District features sensor-driven ventilation , using high-volume , low-speed fans in public areas that are automatically activated by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ : JTC ) The industrial park at CleanTech Three at the Jurong Innovation District features 25 terrace gardens and 11 communal rooftop gardens . ( Photo : JTC ) <p> Over at the Jurong Innovation District , the nine-building industrial park at CleanTech Three , also master planned and designed by JTC , features 25 terrace gardens and 11 communal rooftop gardens . <p> Meanwhile , Mr Praveen Hassan Chandrashekar , Surbana Jurong ( SJ ) Group 's director of sustainability and resilience , said that the urban consulting firm had set out to minimise direct solar radiation through the facade , roof and fenestration when designing its new campus . <p> The result is SJ Campus ' inverted stepped-pyramid form , designed to shade itself while maximising daylight . More than 60 per cent of its building surface faces the north-south direction , reducing east-west sun exposure , while its atrium roof uses triple-layer ethylene tetrafluoroethylene ( ETFE ) , a fluorine-based plastic material that filters ultraviolet ( UV ) rays and cuts solar radiation . <p> Beyond " passive " design , SJ Campus also uses " active @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ one of Singapore 's largest underfloor air distribution systems . <p> The distribution system delivers low-velocity air only to the occupied zone , which maintains a temperature of around 26 degrees Celsius and allows warm air to rise in the unoccupied areas of the atrium higher up , said Mr Praveen . <p> The inverted stepped-pyramid form at Surbana Jurong ( SJ ) Campus is designed to shade itself while maximising daylight . ( Photo : CNA/Ooi Boon Keong ) Mr Tsubasa Bolt , a senior environmentally sustainable design consultant at SJ Group , holding one of many smart wireless diffusers that are part of the under floor air distribution system which controls the airflow rate on the research floor at SJ Campus . ( Photo : CNA/Ooi Boon Keong ) <p> For managing director of CapitaLand Development Chew Peet Mun , heat resilience should be viewed as a " baseline principle " for urban development instead of a " design add-on " . <p> A hallmark of the firm , which develops the properties under CapitaLand entities , is its sky terraces and vertical greenery , such as the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Outram . <p> Its Raffles Place skyscraper CapitaSpring boasts a 35-metre-high green oasis and rooftop sky garden , with 55 per cent of the office building perimeter having a double-skin facade and greenery . <p> This double-skin facade combines high-performance glass and greenery to reduce the energy required for cooling while allowing ample daylight into the building with minimal glare , said Mr Chew . <p> A more recent project by CapitaLand Development , Geneo at Science Park 1 , includes a 3,000-square-metre all-weather plaza connected to Kent Ridge MRT . The plaza is made out of timber , a low-carbon material , and combines shading strategies , greenery and naturally ventilated ground floors , to keep visitors comfortably cool throughout the day . <p> The 3,000-square-metre plaza at Geneo at Science Park 1 is made out of timber , a low-carbon material , and combines shading strategies , greenery and naturally ventilated ground floors to keep visitors cool . ( Photo : Capitaland ) <h> LIMITS IN REDESIGNING A HUMID CITY <p> Yet for all the promise of bold new infrastructure , these innovations are not without their trade-offs @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ challenges posed by Singapore 's humid climate are further obstacles . <p> In 2024 , Singapore ranked 19th out of 30 cities in the Savills Heat Resilience Index , with Helsinki coming in first and Copenhagen ranking second . The two Nordic cities benefit from rare incidents of extreme temperature and progressive environmental and governance policies . <p> Mr Tony Chan , the planning business leader in Southeast Asia for built environment consultancy Arup , said that Singaporean companies are increasingly acknowledging the risks and impact of rising urban heat . <p> But , he added , Singapore faces several structural challenges when it comes to designing for heat resilience . Singapore 's dense urban form creates an urban heat island effect , a phenomenon where more built-up and densely populated areas are warmer than rural areas . <h> Related : <p> Singapore 's high urban density limits its ability to integrate wind corridors , passive cooling zones and more green spaces and parks into its landscape , especially in higher-density areas such as Orchard and Tanjong Pagar , said Mr Chan . <p> Structural limitations and heritage requirements also @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , green facades or ventilation enhancements , he added . <p> Singapore 's climate also limits the effectiveness of certain solutions . <p> For example , Assoc Prof Le Ferrand from NTU noted that the tropical climate limits the durability of the mycelium or fungi tiles that she and her fellow researchers are developing in the lab . <p> " Our material is entirely biodegradable . We have done tests such as putting the tile on a wall outside in the rain . Singapore is so hot and humid -- so the tile lasts a few months , but then they start to degrade and become something else , " she said . <p> Plus , the team anticipates inertia towards adopting these mycelium tiles as an alternative to the insulation materials commonly used today , which have well-established supply chains . <p> " All the polystyrene things that we want to replace are very , very cheap , so that 's a big disadvantage , because we can not say that our material will be as cheap , " said Assoc Prof Le Ferrand . <p> She added that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ thus incentivising companies to turn to more sustainable alternatives . <p> Each mycelium tile , which takes three to four weeks to grow , currently costs around S$9.25 to produce , though producing in larger quantities could bring costs down further . <p> By comparison , a comparable polystyrene tile currently in the market retails for around S$5 . <p> Agreeing , Mr Chan from Arup said developers may not prioritise thermal resilience features if they do not gain directly from long-term energy savings , which will benefit tenants or future owners more . <p> " Many businesses still see heat as a ' comfort ' issue rather than a business risk , so it 's not internalised into risk registers or capital expenditure planning , " said Mr Chan . <p> Still , the NTU scientists creating the mycelium tiles are not daunted and plan to explore options , such as potentially developing a sustainable rainproof coating that could expand the longevity of the tiles outdoors . <p> The team collaborated with local start-up Myk ? lio to scale up the size of the mycelium tiles and successfully fabricated @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sheltered wall for testing . The team is observing the thermal performance of these tiles and understanding public perception of this new material . <p> " Just coming up with the design and creating the tile in the initial phase was very exciting . I think there is promise , potential , and hopefully we are able to find the right pathway that will be able to help ( build ) a more heat-resilient Singapore , " said Mr Soh . <p> There are also regulatory hurdles . For instance , CapitaLand Development had to factor in extra time when building its condominium complex LyndenWoods in Singapore Science Park , because it had to consult the Urban Redevelopment Authority over the sky terraces that were part of the design . <p> But once built , these features have shown results : at the recently revamped CQ @ Clarke Quay , green elements have contributed to the building achieving energy savings of 1.03 million kilowatt-hour per year -- around the annual electricity consumption of about 240 four-room HDB flats . <p> Mr Praveen from SJ Group said that passive design strategies @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ devices , do require higher capital expenditure than conventional approaches , but he agreed that the long-term benefits outweigh the initial costs . <p> " Tenants and staff have responded positively to greenery and biophilic features , noting enhanced well-being and usability of outdoor spaces . Visitors frequently highlight the comfort of the tropical gardens and other common social areas , which remain thermally comfortable yet energy efficient , " he added . <h> HOW SINGAPOREANS KEEP COOL <p> While infrastructural changes will shape Singapore 's long-term heat resilience , many large-scale innovations may still be years away from mainstream adoption or remain confined to specific estates . <p> In the meantime , coping with the heat for many Singaporeans comes down to day-to-day choices and consumer-level solutions . <p> Dr Fabian Lim , a pioneer in thermoregulation and heat injury research in Singapore , said individual behaviour " forms the foundation of heat resilience " , which is further enhanced by the availability of suitable infrastructure and technologies . <p> The senior lecturer at NTU 's Lee Kong Chian School of Medicine suggested behavioural strategies to cope with hot @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ more indoor activities during hot spells . <p> However , he noted that some solutions like the use of an electric fan alone may not be effective for body cooling when air temperature is near to , or higher than , skin temperature of about 36 degrees Celsius . <p> For Ms Yao the teacher , one of the best gifts she has ever received from her husband is a neck fan that cost close to $300 . <p> " It really feels like a lifesaver . It blows cool air out and it helps to bring down the temperature a lot , but it is expensive . The price tag makes people hesitant about splurging on it , " she said . <p> Others have turned to modifying their homes . Generally , rising temperatures not only result in poorer thermal comfort , but increase the risk of heat-related illnesses such as heat stress and heat exhaustion . Furthermore , heat stress also has economic and social costs , as outdoor activities may be disrupted , " said MSE . <p> As interventions for heat could be specific to the time , day and person have to face . True resilience requires adapting to the heat , experts said , both in redesigning the spaces we live in , and training ourselves to cope with hotter days ahead . <p> Around the world , daily life has shifted to accommodate rising temperatures . In Cambodia , for example , school days have been shortened and Spain has introduced a policy banning certain outdoor work during extreme heat warnings . <p> But such measures , while necessary in some contexts , also come with trade-offs . <p> " The policies taken by countries like Cambodia and Spain to shorten school days and ban outdoor work in mid-day have significant impact on productivity and are driven more by the lack of infrastructure than @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> By contrast , he added , Singapore is in a " much better position " thanks to investments in heat-friendly infrastructure , such as sheltered walkways within community spaces . <p> Still , Dr Lim said more can be done to protect outdoor workers in ways that strike a balance between safety and productivity . <p> These include acclimatising new or returning workers gradually to the heat , assigning lighter tasks during peak hours instead of halting work completely , and providing shaded areas or cooling stations where workers can recover every few hours . <p> Even measures such as providing facilities for mid-day showers or tarpaulin shades could help , he said . <p> " Relying solely on avoidance , like staying indoors and constant air-conditioning , leads to physical deconditioning , lower heat tolerance , a greater susceptibility to future heat stress and increases the risk of other aspects of poor health . We must learn to use heat to our advantage , " he said . <p> But while individuals can build their own resilience , adaptation must also be designed into the urban fabric -- @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are planned for a hotter world , the experts added . <p> Ms Kotchchakorn Varaakhom , an internationally acclaimed landscape architect and vice-chair juror for the President 's Design Award 2025 , said cooling individual buildings with technology , insulation , or air-conditioning can inadvertently worsen outdoor heat by radiating it back into the surroundings . <p> This raises overall energy consumption and social and environmental equity concerns , particularly for vulnerable groups who work outdoors and bear the brunt of increased temperatures , she said . <p> " We must consider the entire urban ecosystem , not just isolated structures , technologies , or materials that benefit only a few , " she said . <h> Related : <p> In Thailand , her firm Landprocessredesigned the country 's When you have multiple buildings in close proximity with varying peak cooling demands , you can aggregate that demand efficiently through a centralised system , " said Mr Yeo . <p> This is what the multinational utility company is doing through its two district cooling plants at Punggol Digital District . @@128732013 <h> Once an early mover on ISSB adoption , the city-state has pushed back disclosures for smaller listed firms by up to five years . Regulators cite economic uncertainty , but market watchers warn the move could spur easing of rules regionally . <p> Singapore was among the first in Asia to propose mandatory International Sustainability Standards Board ( ISSB ) -aligned climate reporting for listed and large non-listed companies in 2023 . However , the city-state 's announced delay now puts it behind others in the region , like Australia and Malaysia . Image : Gabrielle See / Eco-Business <p> Singapore has announced a delay in mandatory International Sustainability Standards Board ( ISSB ) -aligned climate reporting for most listed firms by up to five years , in a move that risks undermining its reputation as an early mover in the region . <p> While all Singapore-listed firms were initially required to start making climate disclosures by 2025 , under the revised rules , non-Straits Times Index ( STI ) issuers with a market capitalisation of S$1 billion ( US$780 million ) and above will only be required to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ market capitalisation will be expected to follow from 2030 . <p> Over 550 listed companies -- over 90 per cent of firms listed on the Singapore Exchange ( SGX ) -- will be affected by the delay . <p> Singapore 's revised rules now puts the city-state -- which was among the first in Asia to propose mandatory ISSB-aligned reporting for companies in 2023 -- behind Malaysia in mandatory sustainability reporting requirements . Listed and large non-listed firms in the neighbouring Southeast Asian nation are expected to start ISSB-aligned reporting , including on Scope 3 emissions , by 2027 . <p> While reporting Scope 3 emissions remains mandatory for STI constituents -- the largest SGX-listed issuers -- by 2026 , it will become voluntary until further notice for other listed issuers . <p> As of 30 June , the STI consists of 27 companies , including all three Singapore banks DBS , OCBC and UOB , as well as real estate giants like CapitaLand Investment and CDL . <p> Scope 3 emissions , or fullvalue chain emissions , represents the largest source of emissions for companies . Larger firms heavily @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ smaller in size , to accurately report on their Scope 3 emissions . <p> All SGX-listed issuers are still expected to start reporting on their Scope 1 and 2 emissions -- their direct and indirect emissions from the consumption of purchased electricity , respectively -- by 2025 . However , external limited assurance for these reported emissions has been deferred by two years to 2029 . <p> The timeline revision comes after calls by the Singapore Business Federation ( SBF ) , a group representing the interests of Singaporean businesses , for a one- to two-year extension for smaller listed companies to comply with the mandatory reporting rules in June , following engagements with close to 40 companies . <p> In a joint press release by the Accounting and Corporate Regulatory Authority ( ACRA ) and SGX Regulation ( SGX RegCo ) , the regulatory agencies cited economic uncertainty and readiness gaps among smaller firms for the delay . The aim of the extension is to give these issuers time to build up data collection processes and learn from larger companies that have already embarked on ISSB-aligned climate reporting . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ mandatory start-dates , SGX RegCo expects issuers to build on their existing climate reporting disclosures and to demonstrate progress towards incorporating the climate-relevant provisions in the ISSB standards , " an SGX RegoCo spokesperson told Eco-Business . " The end-goal is for companies to produce quality and accurate reports . " <p> Some challenges that companies have identified include disclosures on climate scenario analysis as well as current and anticipated financial effects of climate change , the spokesperson added . <p> Chia-Tern Huey Min , ACRA 's chief executive , stated that the latest approach " reflects our commitment to supporting companies through current challenges while maintaining Singapore 's momentum in climate action paving the way for more meaningful and higher quality climate-related disclosures in the long run . " <h> ' De-prioritisation of the sustainability agenda ' <p> While experts say that a delay in implementing ISSB standards is unsurprising , the length of it was unexpected and risks leading other countries in the region to reconsider their own timelines . <p> " Mandatory reporting under the ISSB rules is a massive exercise for any company , and the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , " Charlie Knaggs , decarbonisation partner at sustainability consultancy ERM , told Eco-Business . " Notwithstanding , the length of the delay does indicate a de-prioritisation of the sustainability agenda by the relevant agencies . " <p> " The delay does seem to provide an excuse for other countries to ease their reporting requirements -- particularly when viewed in the context of the recent easing of the Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive ( CSRD ) requirements in the European Union , " said Knaggs . <p> Given that mandatory ISSB-aligned reporting rules are meant to help financiers manage climate-related risks , " delaying the requirements does contribute to an increasing risk profile across the financial system , " he added . <p> Lawrence Loh , director for governance and sustainability at the National University of Singapore Business School , told Eco-Business that given that other countries in the region are " in more dire straits " than Singapore due to the United States ' tariffs , the republic 's decision may prompt a readjustment of their own ISSB adoption timelines . <p> " Nothing is fixed . Even countries like @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ look towards reviewing their timelines , because they are also facing the same problems as us . In fact , for all these countries , their US tariffs are higher than Singapore 's , " said Loh . <p> In response to Eco-Business queries , an IFRS Foundation spokesperson said that following Singapore 's revised rules , it will be liaising with the relevant Singapore authorities to update the country 's jurisdictional profile , which it published for the first time in June as part of efforts to track progress on the global adoption of ISSB rules , which the non-profit oversees . <p> A Singapore-based sustainability professional formerly with one of the relevant regulatory authorities , who declined to be named , said that regionally , the republic 's decision is " sure to be fuel for the sustainability deprioritisation fire . " <p> " I believe we 're entering an era where these switcheroos on sustainability-related regulatory timelines will happen more frequently , " especially against the backdrop of the EU 's backpedalling on sustainability policies , including the CSRD , Corporate Sustainability Due Diligence Directive ( CSDDD @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ said . <p> The " last-minute , haphazard manner " in which Singapore 's decision was delivered risks eroding confidence in regulatory efficiency , he added . <h> More clarity needed in next five years <p> Industry players said it remains unclear what Singapore 's five-year delay for the majority of listed issuers is meant to achieve . <p> Using the time to simplify reporting rules and improve disclosure quality , as the EU is attempting with its Omnibus package , could make the delay worthwhile , said Benjamin Soh , co-founder and managing director of ESGpedia , a Singapore-based sustainability reporting platform . <p> Last year , Singapore rolled out a sustainability reporting grant to offset up to a third of the costs of manpower training and consultancy services for companies developing their first ISSB-aligned reports . But Enterprise Singapore , one of the government agencies administering the grant , did not disclose its take-up rate thus far to Eco-Business , calling it " a relatively new scheme . " <p> Soh noted that the delay is unlikely to reduce the demand for carbon accounting services that platforms like @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ us are narrowly focused on ISSB-aligned climate disclosures . The role of a carbon accounting platform is to streamline data all in one place , because businesses will have to calculate their emissions either way , even without ISSB implementation , for procurement , clients or tenders . @@128732015 <p> Manila , 28 August : -- The Philippines will require new and clear strategies to mobilise private sector investment to meet the multi-billion-dollar cost of its nationally determined contributions ( NDCs ) , said leading decision-makers at the Unlocking capital for sustainabilityconference in Manila . <p> The Southeast Asian nation will require a total US$72 billion in investments from 2020 to 2030 to meet its NDC commitments , covering key sectors such as energy , agriculture and transport . Failing to do so could result in annual losses of PHP645 billion ( US$8 billion ) across these key sectors . <p> Dr. Noralene Uy , Assistant Secretary for Policy , Planning , International Affairs and Climate Change for the Department of Environment and Natural Resources ( DENR ) , who was guest-of-honour at the conference , said the Philippines will gradually lose eligibility for concessional loans and grants from multilateral institutions as the country seeks to become an upper middle-income economy by 2026 -- though it would strengthen its creditworthiness at the same time . <p> She said 2025 is a " critical year " for the country as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ goals under the AmBisyon Natin 2040 development plan while addressing escalating climate risks . <p> " A national adaptation investment strategy will be developed to mobilise the resources needed for adaptation programmes , including from the private sector , " she said in her keynote address at the sustainability summit held in Marco Polo Ortigas Manila . <p> Rachel Anne Herrera , Commissioner for the Climate Change Commission ( CCC ) , said the commission is working to strengthen emissions reduction pathways and carbon market readiness as part of the Philippines ' updated NDC -- targeted for submission by the COP30 climate talks in Brazil in November . <p> Herrerra stressed the importance of the private sector in bridging funding gaps and scaling government-led initiatives . <p> " This is where our partnerships with the private sector are instrumental , especially where there are gaps in technology , capital , innovation and creativity . The government 's efforts can be multiplied tenfold with the convening power of industry and financial institutions , and the collective drive of civil society to ensure inclusivity in each step we take , " she @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ will be vital as the Philippines assumes Asean chairmanship next year , prioritising climate change , biodiversity , the blue economy and pollution as key themes for the region . <p> " With its scaling of renewable energy to accelerate the clean energy transition , coupled with commitments to advance climate resiliency , financial inclusion and digital literacy , the Philippines occupies a unique position to lead the region 's vision for 2026 while delivering on its own long-term national development , " said Jessica Cheam , Founder and CEO for Eco-Business . <p> " The challenge will be implementing the right national policies and governance frameworks , getting all stakeholders on board -- from governments to industry and civic society groups , and blending public and private capital to fund sustainable infrastructure and green projects , " she said . <p> Themed ? @@128737666 <p> SINGAPORE : To protect the low-lying areas in Changi and the Greater Southern Waterfront from flooding , a study has proposed building coastal protection structures to form a " continuous line of defence " , with construction set to begin in the 2030s , national water agency PUB said on Saturday ( Aug 30 ) . <p> These include three coastal barriers linking Sentosa , Pulau Brani and the mainland , retrofits to the Marina Barrage and raised coastal protection structures , among other measures . <p> Setback measures , which refer to coastal protection structures built further inland , will also be used at Changi Beach Park so as to preserve beach access and minimise impact on biodiversity . <p> The coastal protection site-specific study for the City-East Coast region started in 2021 and cost S$18 million ( US$14 million ) . The region includes the Greater Southern Waterfront , East Coast and Changi . <p> PUB , along with relevant government agencies , will next look into detailed studies to develop engineering designs of the recommended measures and conduct environmental impact assessments , the agency said . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in the 2030s . <p> The recommendations for the Greater Southern Waterfront and Changi complement other coastal protection efforts at East Coast , providing a continuous line of defence to protect the area against rising sea levels , PUB said . <p> This would include the " Long Island " that was announced in 2023 , which would create an enclosed waterbody in front of East Coast Park and would also serve as a barricade against erosion in the mainland . <p> Speaking at the launch of the Coastal Protection Exhibition on Saturday at VivoCity , Deputy Prime Minister and Minister for Trade and Industry Gan Kim Yong said that unlike larger countries , Singapore does not have the option of " retreating " inland . <p> " Protecting our coastlines is therefore of existential importance , " said Mr Gan , adding that coastal protection is a complex task that will take decades to complete . <p> " But we can take heart in our unique ability to plan far ahead ... Coastal protection is another such endeavour that will require sustained efforts over generations -- and just as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is a necessary investment in the safety , security and resilience of our nation . " <h> The need for coastal protection <p> At this year 's Budget , Prime Minister Lawrence Wong announced that an additional S$5 billion will be injected into the Coastal and Flood Protection Fund . The fund was announced in 2020 with an initial fund of S$5 billion to protect Singapore against rising sea levels . <p> As a low-lying country , about a third of Singapore 's land is less than five metres above mean sea level . <p> Studies from the Centre for Climate Research Singapore and the third National Climate Change Study projected that Singapore could see a permanent mean sea level rise of up to 1.15 metres by 2100 . <p> This means that low-lying areas of the country will be vulnerable to the effects of permanent mean sea level rise , tides and storm surges . <p> If no action is taken , widespread flooding could happen in the City-East Coast region during extreme coastal storm surges in 2050 . <p> Collapse Expand <h> PROTECTING THE GREATER SOUTHERN WATERFRONT <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ mixed-use waterfront precinct with leisure and tourism developments , said Mr Gan . <p> The area stretches from Pasir Panjang Ferry Terminal to Marina East , and includes Sentosa and Pulau Brani , which will be developed into a tourism destination as part of the Greater Sentosa Master Plan . <p> From the study , three coastal barriers and shoreline measures are recommended . <p> Two coastal barriers will link eastern Sentosa to Pulau Brani and then to mainland Singapore , based on a map provided by PUB . The third barrier will connect western Sentosa to mainland Singapore , although the exact location has yet to be determined . <p> These coastal barriers comprise a series of gates that remain open by default to minimise environmental impact and allow maritime activities to take place . During high tides and storm surges , the gates will close to protect the area against coastal flooding . <p> Similar barriers can be found in other countries , such as the Thames Barrier in the United Kingdom and the Maeslant Barrier in the Netherlands . <p> PUB had considered the alternative approach of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rocks , and constructing tidal gates along the waterfront promenade . <p> A tidal gate is typically placed across a water channel , such as a canal or a large outlet drain . It consists of barriers that can be lifted or lowered to control the flow of water . <p> However , such an approach would block sea views and would require more land , PUB said . <p> As part of its study , the agency had considered feedback from stakeholders , including experts , nature groups and youth about preserving direct access to the sea and waterfront promenade . <p> Mr Gan said the construction of coastal barriers will allow the public to still have access to the coastline and enjoy the sea view , which was highlighted by several participants during these feedback sessions . <p> An artist 's impression of a coastal barrier . ( Image : National water agency PUB ) An artist 's impression of a tidal gate at Changi . ( Image : National water agency PUB ) <p> Meanwhile , existing coastal protection along Pasir Panjang Ferry Terminal to Labrador Nature @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be upgraded , PUB said . <p> This includes raising existing sloped protective structures built along shorelines , known as revetments , at Marina South . <p> The Marina Barrage will also be retrofitted to guard against rising sea levels . <h> A MIX OF MEASURES IN CHANGI <p> As for Changi 's coastline , PUB said the study recommended a mix of revetments , tidal gates and bunds as protection measures . A bund is a raised coastal defence structure made from compacted soil , clay or sand . <p> Changi Beach Park could see raised bunds , which can double up as footpaths or cycling tracks . <p> An artist 's impression of a raised setback bund at Changi Beach Park . ( Image : National water agency PUB ) An artist 's impression of the cross-section of an extended revetment at Changi South . ( Image : National water agency PUB ) <p> To preserve recreational access to Changi Beach Park , these bunds will be positioned further inland . <p> Doing so will not only maintain beach access , it will also serve to minimise impact @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , there are plans to install tidal gates at locations where existing major canals discharge to the sea . PUB said that the location and design of such gates will need to be further studied so they integrate with the landscape . <p> PUB will also carry out measures at the shoreline . At Changi South , existing revetments and seawalls may be raised , with the potential of incorporating design features to add recreational value to the area , PUB said . <p> " Coastal protection requires long-term and careful planning , and trade-offs are inevitable . There are still coastlines with unique characteristics that we will need to conduct site-specific studies progressively , " she said , adding that PUB will explore solutions that balance diverse priorities . <p> Site-specific studies at other regions of Singapore 's coastline are ongoing or will commence soon , PUB said . They include site-specific studies at Sentosa Island and the southwestern coast , which are slated to commence in 2026 . <p> The Ministry of Sustainability and Environment and PUB will also introduce a Coastal Protection Bill next year , to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ responsibilities of stakeholders involved . <p> PUB will also launch a code of practice for coastal protection by the first half of 2026 to set standards for the design , construction and operation of coastal protection structures in Singapore .
